Those were the things that usually helped me decompress after work and made me feel like I wasn’t just working and sleeping.

But lately I just… don’t. I go home, sit on the couch, and everything feels like too much effort. Even when I think, “Maybe I should go to yoga,” it doesn’t feel appealing the way it used to. It feels like a chore before I even start. And I’ve been ignoring texts too, not because I’m mad at anyone, but because responding or making plans just feels like more than I can do, and none of it feels worth it right now.

A lot of it feels tied to it, honestly… but not in a neat, direct way where every time I think about the pregnancy, I get sad, and if I don’t think about it, I’m fine. It’s more like since finding out, this heaviness has been sitting on me almost all day.

Yes… definitely. That’s been happening a lot, and it’s honestly scaring me a little because at work I’m usually very careful and pretty quick with routine things.

Lately my brain just feels foggy, like I’m trying to think through cotton or something. I’ll read a medication order and then realize I didn’t actually take it in, so I read it again… and then sometimes again, because I don’t trust that I understood it the first time. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.

And decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? Should I check again?” It takes me longer than it should, and I feel really frustrated with myself because I’m used to being competent and steady with that kind of thing.

It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it feels like I’m carrying something around constantly.\n\nI’m still going to work and doing what I’m supposed to do — I’m a nurse, so I’m used to putting on a pleasant face and smiling with patients — but inside I feel like I’m barely keeping it together. Like I can look normal on the outside, but underneath I feel really sad and weighed down most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think it did kind of start after something specific. It’s been about three weeks now, maybe a little over, and it was shortly after I found out I was pregnant.\n\nI don’t know if I can say it was exactly that moment, like a switch flipped, but looking back the timing is pretty close. I found out, and then not long after that this sadness just sort of settled in and hasn’t really lifted. It’s been there most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Does this sadness feel mainly like a reaction to finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe partly, yeah. The timing lines up so closely that it’s hard not to connect the two. It’s been about three weeks, starting not long after I found out I was pregnant, and since then the sadness has just kind of stayed there.\n\nI don’t know if it’s only because of the pregnancy, or if finding out just brought something up in me, but that’s when I really noticed it starting. It wasn’t like I felt this way for months before and then happened to find out — it really began shortly after that.\n\nDoctor: Does finding out you’re pregnant feel like the main reason you’ve been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. It’s the thing I keep circling back to because the timing is so close, so part of me feels like… maybe, yes, it has something to do with it. But I don’t know that I can say it’s the main reason with confidence.\n\nIt’s more like finding out I’m pregnant happened, and then this heavy feeling came in afterward, and now I’m trying to make sense of it. I keep asking myself, “Is this because of the pregnancy? Is it fear? Is it something else?” And I don’t really have a clean answer. It feels connected, but I can’t tell whether it’s the whole cause or just what made me notice how bad I was feeling.\n\nDoctor: Before this started three weeks ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve had anything that felt exactly like this before, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nI’ve had stressful times, of course, and times where I felt down or overwhelmed, but this feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, this happened before.” I’m trying to think back, and nothing is really standing out to me as a period where I felt this same kind of sadness for days and days like this. So… I guess my answer is no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the pregnancy is what’s causing this sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. It feels connected, because it started around the same time, and that makes it really hard for me not to link the two in my head. But saying, “Yes, the pregnancy is causing this,” feels too definite, and I don’t feel that certain.\n\nIt’s more like… I found out, and then afterward this sadness was there, and now I keep trying to figure out whether one caused the other or whether it just brought something to the surface. I wish I had a clearer answer, but I really don’t. It feels related, but I can’t honestly say I know it’s the cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. That’s actually been one of the things that feels really unlike me.\n\nAfter my shifts, I used to have things I’d do to sort of come back to myself, you know? Like I would paint sometimes, even if it was just something small, or I’d go to yoga, or salsa class if I had the energy. Those were the things that usually helped me decompress after work and made me feel like I wasn’t just working and sleeping.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t. I go home, sit on the couch, and everything feels like too much effort. Even when I think, “Maybe I should go to yoga,” it doesn’t feel appealing the way it used to. It feels like a chore before I even start. And I’ve been ignoring texts too, not because I’m mad at anyone, but because responding or making plans just feels like more than I can do, and none of it feels worth it right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past three weeks, had you been feeling depressed or down most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I was stressed or tired or just not feeling great, especially with work and life stuff, but not depressed or down most days for two years.\n\nThat sounds like… a really long, ongoing thing, and I don’t think that was me before these last few weeks. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say something too quickly, but when I think back over the past couple years, I wouldn’t describe myself as feeling like this most days. This feels much more recent.\n\nDoctor: How much of your sadness feels directly tied to thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: A lot of it feels tied to it, honestly… but not in a neat, direct way where every time I think about the pregnancy, I get sad, and if I don’t think about it, I’m fine. It’s more like since finding out, this heaviness has been sitting on me almost all day.\n\nI came in because for the past few weeks I’ve felt this heavy, sad pressure on me, like it’s just there in the background no matter what I’m doing. Even at work, I can be smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look okay, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo when I think about the pregnancy, yes, it can make that feeling sharper or more real, I guess. But the sadness doesn’t completely switch off when I’m not actively thinking about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s been happening a lot, and it’s honestly scaring me a little because at work I’m usually very careful and pretty quick with routine things.\n\nLately my brain just feels foggy, like I’m trying to think through cotton or something. I’ll read a medication order and then realize I didn’t actually take it in, so I read it again… and then sometimes again, because I don’t trust that I understood it the first time. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.\n\nAnd decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? I mean, I’ve had times where I was stressed or tired or just not feeling great, especially with work and life stuff, but not depressed or down most days for two years.\n\nThat sounds like… a really long, ongoing thing, and I don’t think that was me before these last few weeks. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say something too quickly, but when I think back over the past couple years, I wouldn’t describe myself as feeling like this most days. This feels much more recent.\n\nDoctor: How much of your sadness feels directly tied to thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: A lot of it feels tied to it, honestly… but not in a neat, direct way where every time I think about the pregnancy, I get sad, and if I don’t think about it, I’m fine. It’s more like since finding out, this heaviness has been sitting on me almost all day.\n\nI came in because for the past few weeks I’ve felt this heavy, sad pressure on me, like it’s just there in the background no matter what I’m doing. Even at work, I can be smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look okay, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo when I think about the pregnancy, yes, it can make that feeling sharper or more real, I guess. But the sadness doesn’t completely switch off when I’m not actively thinking about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s been happening a lot, and it’s honestly scaring me a little because at work I’m usually very careful and pretty quick with routine things.\n\nLately my brain just feels foggy, like I’m trying to think through cotton or something. I’ll read a medication order and then realize I didn’t actually take it in, so I read it again… and then sometimes again, because I don’t trust that I understood it the first time. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.\n\nAnd decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? Should I check again?” It takes me longer than it should, and I feel really frustrated with myself because I’m used to being competent and steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Does the intensity of this sadness feel stronger than you would expect just from finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep asking myself that, like, “Is this just because I found out I’m pregnant? Is this a normal reaction?” And I know that’s a big thing to take in, so I don’t want to make it sound like it has nothing to do with it.\n\nBut the level of sadness feels… bigger than that, I guess. It’s been there most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Does this sadness feel mainly like a reaction to finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe partly, yeah. The timing lines up so closely that it’s hard not to connect the two. It’s been about three weeks, starting not long after I found out I was pregnant, and since then the sadness has just kind of stayed there.\n\nI don’t know if it’s only because of the pregnancy, or if finding out just brought something up in me, but that’s when I really noticed it starting. It wasn’t like I felt this way for months before and then happened to find out — it really began shortly after that.\n\nDoctor: Does finding out you’re pregnant feel like the main reason you’ve been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. It’s the thing I keep circling back to because the timing is so close, so part of me feels like… maybe, yes, it has something to do with it. But I don’t know that I can say it’s the main reason with confidence.\n\nIt’s more like finding out I’m pregnant happened, and then this heavy feeling came in afterward, and now I’m trying to make sense of it. I keep asking myself, “Is this because of the pregnancy? Is it fear? Is it something else?” And I don’t really have a clean answer. It feels connected, but I can’t tell whether it’s the whole cause or just what made me notice how bad I was feeling.\n\nDoctor: Before this started three weeks ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve had anything that felt exactly like this before, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nI’ve had stressful times, of course, and times where I felt down or overwhelmed, but this feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, this happened before.” I’m trying to think back, and nothing is really standing out to me as a period where I felt this same kind of sadness for days and days like this. So… I guess my answer is no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the pregnancy is what’s causing this sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. It feels connected, because it started around the same time, and that makes it really hard for me not to link the two in my head. But saying, “Yes, the pregnancy is causing this,” feels too definite, and I don’t feel that certain.\n\nIt’s more like… I found out, and then afterward this sadness was there, and now I keep trying to figure out whether one caused the other or whether it just brought something to the surface. I wish I had a clearer answer, but I really don’t. It feels related, but I can’t honestly say I know it’s the cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. That’s actually been one of the things that feels really unlike me.\n\nAfter my shifts, I used to have things I’d do to sort of come back to myself, you know? Like I would paint sometimes, even if it was just something small, or I’d go to yoga, or salsa class if I had the energy. Those were the things that usually helped me decompress after work and made me feel like I wasn’t just working and sleeping.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t. I go home, sit on the couch, and everything feels like too much effort. Even when I think, “Maybe I should go to yoga,” it doesn’t feel appealing the way it used to. It feels like a chore before I even start. And I’ve been ignoring texts too, not because I’m mad at anyone, but because responding or making plans just feels like more than I can do, and none of it feels worth it right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past three weeks, had you been feeling depressed or down most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I was stressed or tired or just not feeling great, especially with work and life stuff, but not depressed or down most days for two years.\n\nThat sounds like… a really long, ongoing thing, and I don’t think that was me before these last few weeks. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say something too quickly, but when I think back over the past couple years, I wouldn’t describe myself as feeling like this most days. This feels much more recent.\n\nDoctor: How much of your sadness feels directly tied to thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: A lot of it feels tied to it, honestly… but not in a neat, direct way where every time I think about the pregnancy, I get sad, and if I don’t think about it, I’m fine. It’s more like since finding out, this heaviness has been sitting on me almost all day.\n\nI came in because for the past few weeks I’ve felt this heavy, sad pressure on me, like it’s just there in the background no matter what I’m doing. Even at work, I can be smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look okay, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo when I think about the pregnancy, yes, it can make that feeling sharper or more real, I guess. But the sadness doesn’t completely switch off when I’m not actively thinking about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s been happening a lot, and it’s honestly scaring me a little because at work I’m usually very careful and pretty quick with routine things.\n\nLately my brain just feels foggy, like I’m trying to think through cotton or something. I’ll read a medication order and then realize I didn’t actually take it in, so I read it again… and then sometimes again, because I don’t trust that I understood it the first time. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.\n\nAnd decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? Should I check again?” It takes me longer than it should, and I feel really frustrated with myself because I’m used to being competent and steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Does the intensity of this sadness feel stronger than you would expect just from finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep asking myself that, like, “Is this just because I found out I’m pregnant? Is this a normal reaction?” And I know that’s a big thing to take in, so I don’t want to make it sound like it has nothing to do with it.\n\nBut the level of sadness feels… bigger than that, I guess. Heavier. It’s not just a moment of being upset or overwhelmed when I think about it. For the past few weeks it’s felt like this heavy, sad pressure just sitting on me almost all day. Like it follows me around.\n\nEven when I’m at work and I’m smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to be doing, inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. So yes, I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just finding out. It feels like it’s taken over more of me than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day nearly every day, even when you’re not thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, nearly every day. It’s not just when I’m actively thinking about the pregnancy. That can definitely bring it up more sharply, but even when my mind is on something else, there’s still this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because for the past few weeks it’s felt like it’s there almost all day. I’ll be at work, smiling at patients, answering questions, doing what I’m supposed to do, and I think from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m carrying this weight around while trying to act like everything is fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my sleep closely enough to give you a clear answer, and I don’t want to say there’s been a definite change if I’m just guessing.\n\nIt hasn’t stood out to me as the main thing, I guess. Like, when I think about what’s been feeling wrong, sleep isn’t the first thing that comes to mind. But I also haven’t really stopped and paid attention to whether it’s different from my normal. So… I’d say I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

So… I’d say I’m not sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling worthless or unusually guilty lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 28 / licensed_practical_or_licensed_vocational_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I’ve been trying to sort that out, because “worthless” feels like a really strong word. I don’t know that I would say I feel worthless exactly.

I do feel… disappointed in myself sometimes, or like I should be handling things better than I am. Like I’ll catch myself thinking, “You’re a nurse, you take care of other people, you’re supposed to be able to keep it together,” and then I feel bad that I can’t just push through it. But I’m not sure if that’s guilt in the way you mean it.

It’s more like I’m judging myself, I guess. Like I should be stronger or more grateful or more clear-headed. But I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I’m a worthless person.” It’s just… I don’t feel like myself, and then I feel bad about not feeling like myself.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, thank you. I guess I came in because for the past few weeks I’ve just felt this really heavy sadness, like a pressure sitting on me almost all day. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it feels like I’m carrying something around constantly.\n\nI’m still going to work and doing what I’m supposed to do — I’m a nurse, so I’m used to putting on a pleasant face and smiling with patients — but inside I feel like I’m barely keeping it together. Like I can look normal on the outside, but underneath I feel really sad and weighed down most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think it did kind of start after something specific. It’s been about three weeks now, maybe a little over, and it was shortly after I found out I was pregnant.\n\nI don’t know if I can say it was exactly that moment, like a switch flipped, but looking back the timing is pretty close. I found out, and then not long after that this sadness just sort of settled in and hasn’t really lifted. It’s been there most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Does this sadness feel mainly like a reaction to finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe partly, yeah. The timing lines up so closely that it’s hard not to connect the two. It’s been about three weeks, starting not long after I found out I was pregnant, and since then the sadness has just kind of stayed there.\n\nI don’t know if it’s only because of the pregnancy, or if finding out just brought something up in me, but that’s when I really noticed it starting. It wasn’t like I felt this way for months before and then happened to find out — it really began shortly after that.\n\nDoctor: Does finding out you’re pregnant feel like the main reason you’ve been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. It’s the thing I keep circling back to because the timing is so close, so part of me feels like… maybe, yes, it has something to do with it. But I don’t know that I can say it’s the main reason with confidence.\n\nIt’s more like finding out I’m pregnant happened, and then this heavy feeling came in afterward, and now I’m trying to make sense of it. I keep asking myself, “Is this because of the pregnancy? Is it fear? Is it something else?” And I don’t really have a clean answer. It feels connected, but I can’t tell whether it’s the whole cause or just what made me notice how bad I was feeling.\n\nDoctor: Before this started three weeks ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve had anything that felt exactly like this before, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nI’ve had stressful times, of course, and times where I felt down or overwhelmed, but this feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, this happened before.” I’m trying to think back, and nothing is really standing out to me as a period where I felt this same kind of sadness for days and days like this. So… I guess my answer is no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the pregnancy is what’s causing this sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. It feels connected, because it started around the same time, and that makes it really hard for me not to link the two in my head. But saying, “Yes, the pregnancy is causing this,” feels too definite, and I don’t feel that certain.\n\nIt’s more like… I found out, and then afterward this sadness was there, and now I keep trying to figure out whether one caused the other or whether it just brought something to the surface. I wish I had a clearer answer, but I really don’t. It feels related, but I can’t honestly say I know it’s the cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. That’s actually been one of the things that feels really unlike me.\n\nAfter my shifts, I used to have things I’d do to sort of come back to myself, you know? Like I would paint sometimes, even if it was just something small, or I’d go to yoga, or salsa class if I had the energy. Those were the things that usually helped me decompress after work and made me feel like I wasn’t just working and sleeping.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t. I go home, sit on the couch, and everything feels like too much effort. Even when I think, “Maybe I should go to yoga,” it doesn’t feel appealing the way it used to. It feels like a chore before I even start. And I’ve been ignoring texts too, not because I’m mad at anyone, but because responding or making plans just feels like more than I can do, and none of it feels worth it right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past three weeks, had you been feeling depressed or down most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I was stressed or tired or just not feeling great, especially with work and life stuff, but not depressed or down most days for two years.\n\nThat sounds like… a really long, ongoing thing, and I don’t think that was me before these last few weeks. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say something too quickly, but when I think back over the past couple years, I wouldn’t describe myself as feeling like this most days. This feels much more recent.\n\nDoctor: How much of your sadness feels directly tied to thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: A lot of it feels tied to it, honestly… but not in a neat, direct way where every time I think about the pregnancy, I get sad, and if I don’t think about it, I’m fine. It’s more like since finding out, this heaviness has been sitting on me almost all day.\n\nI came in because for the past few weeks I’ve felt this heavy, sad pressure on me, like it’s just there in the background no matter what I’m doing. Even at work, I can be smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look okay, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo when I think about the pregnancy, yes, it can make that feeling sharper or more real, I guess. But the sadness doesn’t completely switch off when I’m not actively thinking about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s been happening a lot, and it’s honestly scaring me a little because at work I’m usually very careful and pretty quick with routine things.\n\nLately my brain just feels foggy, like I’m trying to think through cotton or something. I’ll read a medication order and then realize I didn’t actually take it in, so I read it again… and then sometimes again, because I don’t trust that I understood it the first time. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.\n\nAnd decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? Should I check again?” It takes me longer than it should, and I feel really frustrated with myself because I’m used to being competent and steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Does the intensity of this sadness feel stronger than you would expect just from finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep asking myself that, like, “Is this just because I found out I’m pregnant? Is this a normal reaction?” And I know that’s a big thing to take in, so I don’t want to make it sound like it has nothing to do with it.\n\nBut the level of sadness feels… bigger than that, I guess. Like it follows me around.\n\nEven when I’m at work and I’m smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to be doing, inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. So yes, I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just finding out. It feels like it’s taken over more of me than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day nearly every day, even when you’re not thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, nearly every day. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it has, even though I’m still showing up and doing my shifts. I haven’t called out or anything, and I keep telling myself that means I’m functioning, but it’s not the same as before.

At work, I’m getting through the day, but it takes so much more effort to stay on top of everything. My supervisor has already noticed that some of my charting hasn’t been finished the way it should be, which felt really embarrassing. I’m usually very careful about that, so having someone point it out made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just in my head. It’s actually affecting how I’m doing my job.

And outside of work, I’ve been pulling back a lot. Friends will text me and I’ll see it and think I should answer, and then I just… don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too awkward. I’m also not really doing the things that usually keep me steady, like staying connected with people or keeping up with my normal routines. I still manage the basic responsibilities, but it feels like I’m doing the bare minimum and slowly letting the rest slip.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, thank you. I guess I came in because for the past few weeks I’ve just felt this really heavy sadness, like a pressure sitting on me almost all day. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it feels like I’m carrying something around constantly.\n\nI’m still going to work and doing what I’m supposed to do — I’m a nurse, so I’m used to putting on a pleasant face and smiling with patients — but inside I feel like I’m barely keeping it together. Like I can look normal on the outside, but underneath I feel really sad and weighed down most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think it did kind of start after something specific. It’s been about three weeks now, maybe a little over, and it was shortly after I found out I was pregnant.\n\nI don’t know if I can say it was exactly that moment, like a switch flipped, but looking back the timing is pretty close. I found out, and then not long after that this sadness just sort of settled in and hasn’t really lifted. It’s been there most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Does this sadness feel mainly like a reaction to finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe partly, yeah. The timing lines up so closely that it’s hard not to connect the two. It’s been about three weeks, starting not long after I found out I was pregnant, and since then the sadness has just kind of stayed there.\n\nI don’t know if it’s only because of the pregnancy, or if finding out just brought something up in me, but that’s when I really noticed it starting. It wasn’t like I felt this way for months before and then happened to find out — it really began shortly after that.\n\nDoctor: Does finding out you’re pregnant feel like the main reason you’ve been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. It’s the thing I keep circling back to because the timing is so close, so part of me feels like… maybe, yes, it has something to do with it. But I don’t know that I can say it’s the main reason with confidence.\n\nIt’s more like finding out I’m pregnant happened, and then this heavy feeling came in afterward, and now I’m trying to make sense of it. I keep asking myself, “Is this because of the pregnancy? Is it fear? Is it something else?” And I don’t really have a clean answer. It feels connected, but I can’t tell whether it’s the whole cause or just what made me notice how bad I was feeling.\n\nDoctor: Before this started three weeks ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve had anything that felt exactly like this before, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nI’ve had stressful times, of course, and times where I felt down or overwhelmed, but this feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, this happened before.” I’m trying to think back, and nothing is really standing out to me as a period where I felt this same kind of sadness for days and days like this. So… I guess my answer is no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the pregnancy is what’s causing this sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. It feels connected, because it started around the same time, and that makes it really hard for me not to link the two in my head. But saying, “Yes, the pregnancy is causing this,” feels too definite, and I don’t feel that certain.\n\nIt’s more like… I found out, and then afterward this sadness was there, and now I keep trying to figure out whether one caused the other or whether it just brought something to the surface. I wish I had a clearer answer, but I really don’t. It feels related, but I can’t honestly say I know it’s the cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. That’s actually been one of the things that feels really unlike me.\n\nAfter my shifts, I used to have things I’d do to sort of come back to myself, you know? Like I would paint sometimes, even if it was just something small, or I’d go to yoga, or salsa class if I had the energy. Those were the things that usually helped me decompress after work and made me feel like I wasn’t just working and sleeping.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t. I go home, sit on the couch, and everything feels like too much effort. Even when I think, “Maybe I should go to yoga,” it doesn’t feel appealing the way it used to. It feels like a chore before I even start. And I’ve been ignoring texts too, not because I’m mad at anyone, but because responding or making plans just feels like more than I can do, and none of it feels worth it right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past three weeks, had you been feeling depressed or down most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I was stressed or tired or just not feeling great, especially with work and life stuff, but not depressed or down most days for two years.\n\nThat sounds like… a really long, ongoing thing, and I don’t think that was me before these last few weeks. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say something too quickly, but when I think back over the past couple years, I wouldn’t describe myself as feeling like this most days. This feels much more recent.\n\nDoctor: How much of your sadness feels directly tied to thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: A lot of it feels tied to it, honestly… but not in a neat, direct way where every time I think about the pregnancy, I get sad, and if I don’t think about it, I’m fine. It’s more like since finding out, this heaviness has been sitting on me almost all day.\n\nI came in because for the past few weeks I’ve felt this heavy, sad pressure on me, like it’s just there in the background no matter what I’m doing. Even at work, I can be smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look okay, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo when I think about the pregnancy, yes, it can make that feeling sharper or more real, I guess. But the sadness doesn’t completely switch off when I’m not actively thinking about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s been happening a lot, and it’s honestly scaring me a little because at work I’m usually very careful and pretty quick with routine things.\n\nLately my brain just feels foggy, like I’m trying to think through cotton or something. I’ll read a medication order and then realize I didn’t actually take it in, so I read it again… and then sometimes again, because I don’t trust that I understood it the first time. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.\n\nAnd decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? Should I check again?” It takes me longer than it should, and I feel really frustrated with myself because I’m used to being competent and steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Does the intensity of this sadness feel stronger than you would expect just from finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep asking myself that, like, “Is this just because I found out I’m pregnant? Is this a normal reaction?” And I know that’s a big thing to take in, so I don’t want to make it sound like it has nothing to do with it.\n\nBut the level of sadness feels… bigger than that, I guess. Heavier. It’s not just a moment of being upset or overwhelmed when I think about it. For the past few weeks it’s felt like this heavy, sad pressure just sitting on me almost all day. Like it follows me around.\n\nEven when I’m at work and I’m smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to be doing, inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. So yes, I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just finding out. It feels like it’s taken over more of me than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day nearly every day, even when you’re not thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, nearly every day. It’s not just when I’m actively thinking about the pregnancy. That can definitely bring it up more sharply, but even when my mind is on something else, there’s still this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because for the past few weeks it’s felt like it’s there almost all day. I’ll be at work, smiling at patients, answering questions, doing what I’m supposed to do, and I think from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m carrying this weight around while trying to act like everything is fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.\n\nAnd decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? Should I check again?” It takes me longer than it should, and I feel really frustrated with myself because I’m used to being competent and steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Does the intensity of this sadness feel stronger than you would expect just from finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep asking myself that, like, “Is this just because I found out I’m pregnant? Is this a normal reaction?” And I know that’s a big thing to take in, so I don’t want to make it sound like it has nothing to do with it.\n\nBut the level of sadness feels… bigger than that, I guess. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I didn’t really have words for it at first, but it feels like everything I do is slowed down, like I’m moving through syrup or something. Not just tired, exactly, but like my body takes longer to start and longer to finish anything.

At the clinic, even simple things that I normally do automatically feel like they take effort. Like gathering wound care supplies — that should be quick, I know where everything is — but I’ll find myself moving so slowly, double-checking, standing there for a second like I’m trying to remember the next step. And charting takes me noticeably longer than it should. I’ll be sitting there trying to finish something that used to feel routine, and it’s like my hands and my brain are both dragging.

I don’t know if other people would see it as me moving in slow motion or if it just feels that way to me, but I definitely notice that I’m not as quick or efficient as I usually am. It’s frustrating because I know how I normally function, and this is not that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, thank you. I guess I came in because for the past few weeks I’ve just felt this really heavy sadness, like a pressure sitting on me almost all day. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it feels like I’m carrying something around constantly.\n\nI’m still going to work and doing what I’m supposed to do — I’m a nurse, so I’m used to putting on a pleasant face and smiling with patients — but inside I feel like I’m barely keeping it together. Like I can look normal on the outside, but underneath I feel really sad and weighed down most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness start after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think it did kind of start after something specific. It’s been about three weeks now, maybe a little over, and it was shortly after I found out I was pregnant.\n\nI don’t know if I can say it was exactly that moment, like a switch flipped, but looking back the timing is pretty close. I found out, and then not long after that this sadness just sort of settled in and hasn’t really lifted. It’s been there most days since then.\n\nDoctor: Does this sadness feel mainly like a reaction to finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe partly, yeah. The timing lines up so closely that it’s hard not to connect the two. It’s been about three weeks, starting not long after I found out I was pregnant, and since then the sadness has just kind of stayed there.\n\nI don’t know if it’s only because of the pregnancy, or if finding out just brought something up in me, but that’s when I really noticed it starting. It wasn’t like I felt this way for months before and then happened to find out — it really began shortly after that.\n\nDoctor: Does finding out you’re pregnant feel like the main reason you’ve been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. It’s the thing I keep circling back to because the timing is so close, so part of me feels like… maybe, yes, it has something to do with it. I keep asking myself, “Is this because of the pregnancy? Is it fear? Is it something else?” And I don’t really have a clean answer. It feels connected, but I can’t tell whether it’s the whole cause or just what made me notice how bad I was feeling.\n\nDoctor: Before this started three weeks ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I’ve had anything that felt exactly like this before, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nI’ve had stressful times, of course, and times where I felt down or overwhelmed, but this feels different enough that I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, this happened before.” I’m trying to think back, and nothing is really standing out to me as a period where I felt this same kind of sadness for days and days like this. So… I guess my answer is no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that the pregnancy is what’s causing this sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. But saying, “Yes, the pregnancy is causing this,” feels too definite, and I don’t feel that certain.\n\nIt’s more like… I found out, and then afterward this sadness was there, and now I keep trying to figure out whether one caused the other or whether it just brought something to the surface. I wish I had a clearer answer, but I really don’t. It feels related, but I can’t honestly say I know it’s the cause.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. That’s actually been one of the things that feels really unlike me.\n\nAfter my shifts, I used to have things I’d do to sort of come back to myself, you know? Like I would paint sometimes, even if it was just something small, or I’d go to yoga, or salsa class if I had the energy. Those were the things that usually helped me decompress after work and made me feel like I wasn’t just working and sleeping.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t. I go home, sit on the couch, and everything feels like too much effort. Even when I think, “Maybe I should go to yoga,” it doesn’t feel appealing the way it used to. It feels like a chore before I even start. And I’ve been ignoring texts too, not because I’m mad at anyone, but because responding or making plans just feels like more than I can do, and none of it feels worth it right now.\n\nDoctor: Before these past three weeks, had you been feeling depressed or down most days for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where I was stressed or tired or just not feeling great, especially with work and life stuff, but not depressed or down most days for two years.\n\nThat sounds like… a really long, ongoing thing, and I don’t think that was me before these last few weeks. I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to say something too quickly, but when I think back over the past couple years, I wouldn’t describe myself as feeling like this most days. This feels much more recent.\n\nDoctor: How much of your sadness feels directly tied to thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: A lot of it feels tied to it, honestly… but not in a neat, direct way where every time I think about the pregnancy, I get sad, and if I don’t think about it, I’m fine. It’s more like since finding out, this heaviness has been sitting on me almost all day.\n\nI came in because for the past few weeks I’ve felt this heavy, sad pressure on me, like it’s just there in the background no matter what I’m doing. Even at work, I can be smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to do, and from the outside I probably look okay, but inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together.\n\nSo when I think about the pregnancy, yes, it can make that feeling sharper or more real, I guess. But the sadness doesn’t completely switch off when I’m not actively thinking about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s been happening a lot, and it’s honestly scaring me a little because at work I’m usually very careful and pretty quick with routine things.\n\nLately my brain just feels foggy, like I’m trying to think through cotton or something. I’ll read a medication order and then realize I didn’t actually take it in, so I read it again… and then sometimes again, because I don’t trust that I understood it the first time. Same with wound-care notes. I’ll be standing there looking at them, and it’s not that I can’t read them, obviously, but it’s like the information doesn’t stick the way it normally does.\n\nAnd decisions that I normally make almost automatically now make me freeze. Like I’ll know what I’m supposed to do, but then I start second-guessing myself — “Did I read that right? Am I remembering the dressing change correctly? Should I check again?” It takes me longer than it should, and I feel really frustrated with myself because I’m used to being competent and steady with that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Does the intensity of this sadness feel stronger than you would expect just from finding out you’re pregnant?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I keep asking myself that, like, “Is this just because I found out I’m pregnant? Is this a normal reaction?” And I know that’s a big thing to take in, so I don’t want to make it sound like it has nothing to do with it.\n\nBut the level of sadness feels… bigger than that, I guess. Heavier. It’s not just a moment of being upset or overwhelmed when I think about it. For the past few weeks it’s felt like this heavy, sad pressure just sitting on me almost all day. Like it follows me around.\n\nEven when I’m at work and I’m smiling at patients and doing what I’m supposed to be doing, inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. So yes, I’d say it feels stronger than what I would expect from just finding out. It feels like it’s taken over more of me than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day nearly every day, even when you’re not thinking about the pregnancy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, nearly every day. It’s not just when I’m actively thinking about the pregnancy. That can definitely bring it up more sharply, but even when my mind is on something else, there’s still this heavy, sad pressure sitting on me.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because for the past few weeks it’s felt like it’s there almost all day. I’ll be at work, smiling at patients, answering questions, doing what I’m supposed to do, and I think from the outside I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel like I’m barely holding myself together. It’s like I’m carrying this weight around while trying to act like everything is fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my sleep closely enough to give you a clear answer, and I don’t want to say there’s been a definite change if I’m just guessing.\n\nIt hasn’t stood out to me as the main thing, I guess. Like, when I think about what’s been feeling wrong, sleep isn’t the first thing that comes to mind. But I also haven’t really stopped and paid attention to whether it’s different from my normal. So… I’d say I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or unusually guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’ve been trying to sort that out, because “worthless” feels like a really strong word. I don’t know that I would say I feel worthless exactly.\n\nI do feel… disappointed in myself sometimes, or like I should be handling things better than I am. Like I’ll catch myself thinking, “You’re a nurse, you take care of other people, you’re supposed to be able to keep it together,” and then I feel bad that I can’t just push through it. But I’m not sure if that’s guilt in the way you mean it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m judging myself, I guess. Like I should be stronger or more grateful or more clear-headed. But I haven’t really thought of it as, like, “I’m a worthless person.” It’s just… I don’t feel like myself, and then I feel bad about not feeling like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings made it hard for you to do your job or manage your usual responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, even though I’m still showing up and doing my shifts. I haven’t called out or anything, and I keep telling myself that means I’m functioning, but it’s not the same as before.\n\nAt work, I’m getting through the day, but it takes so much more effort to stay on top of everything. My supervisor has already noticed that some of my charting hasn’t been finished the way it should be, which felt really embarrassing. I’m usually very careful about that, so having someone point it out made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just in my head. It’s actually affecting how I’m doing my job.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been pulling back a lot. Friends will text me and I’ll see it and think I should answer, and then I just… don’t. Or I put it off until it feels too awkward. I’m also not really doing the things that usually keep me steady, like staying connected with people or keeping up with my normal routines. I still manage the basic responsibilities, but it feels like I’m doing the bare minimum and slowly letting the rest slip.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about wanting to die or hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… um… that’s actually the thing that scared me enough to say something at my prenatal appointment.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself, and I don’t have, like, a specific thing I’m going to do. But I’ve been having thoughts like… I wish I could just not wake up. Like if I went to sleep and somehow didn’t have to keep doing this, there’s a part of me that feels relieved by that thought, and that really scared me when I noticed it.\n\nIt’s hard to say out loud because I don’t want people to think I’m trying to do something or that I’m unsafe every second, but it also doesn’t feel okay to keep pretending it’s nothing. I haven’t been thinking, “I’m going to hurt myself,” exactly. It’s more like I don’t want to be here, or I want everything to stop, and then I feel awful and frightened that I’m even thinking that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t really have words for it at first, but it feels like everything I do is slowed down, like I’m moving through syrup or something. Not just tired, exactly, but like my body takes longer to start and longer to finish anything.\n\nAt the clinic, even simple things that I normally do automatically feel like they take effort. I’ll be sitting there trying to finish something that used to feel routine, and it’s like my hands and my brain are both dragging.\n\nI don’t know if other people would see it as me moving in slow motion or if it just feels that way to me, but I definitely notice that I’m not as quick or efficient as I usually am. It’s frustrating because I know how I normally function, and this is not that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
